In response to complaints from conservative watchdog groups and Republican lawmakers,[^cite_note-\:04-374] Attorney General [John Ashcroft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Ashcroft) instructed federal prosecutors to make voter fraud a priority, launching more than 300 investigations in 2001. Five years later, the Bush Justice Department disclosed that they had charged 119 people with election crimes and convicted just 86 – most of which were people who cast a single ballot incorrectly or had simply registered improperly and didn't vote at all.[^cite_note-\:110-375] Since previous guidelines barred federal prosecutions of "isolated acts of individual wrongdoing" that were not part of schemes to corrupt elections, some prosecutors were uneasy about charging individual voters, leading to a highly publicized [dismissal of several U.S. attorneys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_dismissal_of_U.S._attorneys).[^cite_note-\:04-374]

A Justice Department trial attorney said "It's remarkable that all of the U.S. attorneys had a mandate and were given adequate resources to raise this to the top of the pile. They all agree we found a handful of cases … and that was it."[^cite_note-\:110-375]

### Iowa investigation (2012-2014)

In 2012, Iowa Secretary of State [Matt Schultz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matt_Schultz) ordered a state-wide investigation into voter fraud,[^cite_note-\:22-376] and after a two-year effort, concluded with only 6 convictions,[^cite_note-377] the majority of which were felons who mistakenly believed they were eligible to vote.[^cite_note-\:22-376] An report issued by the investigation claimed that only 117 votes were cast by ineligible voters,[^cite_note-\:32-378] a small fraction of the 1.58 million votes cast in Iowa during the 2012 election,[^cite_note-\:22-376] and did not suggest that the outcomes of any Iowa elections were affected by fraudulent votes.[^cite_note-\:32-378]

### Voter fraud commission (2017)

Main article: [Presidential Advisory Commission on Election Integrity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_Advisory_Commission_on_Election_Integrity)



President Trump signing the Executive Order establishing the Voter Fraud Commission&#x20;

On May 11, 2017, Trump signed an [executive order](210541.md) to establish a commission to conduct an investigation into voter fraud, chaired by Vice President [Mike Pence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Pence) with Kansas Secretary of State [Kris Kobach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kris_Kobach) as vice chair.[^cite_note-politico_2017-379] Kobach and the commission failed to provide evidence for claims of voter fraud by Trump and others on the commission.[^cite_note-\:23-380] Trump's creation of the commission was criticized by [voting rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voting_rights) advocates, scholars and experts, and newspaper editorial boards as a pretext for, and prelude to, [voter suppression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voter_suppression).[^cite_note-WashingtonPost-381][^cite_note-AshCenter-382][^cite_note-Waldman-383][^cite_note-BermanWeigel-384][^cite_note-\:23-380] [Matt Dunlap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matthew_Dunlap), a Democrat on the commission, called it a sham designed to gin up anti-immigrant sentiment.[^cite_note-\:13-15] In January 2018, Trump abruptly disbanded the commission,[^cite_note-TackettWines-385] which met only twice.[^cite_note-Villeneuve-386] The commission found no evidence of widespread voter fraud in the United States.[^cite_note-TackettWines-385][^cite_note-Villeneuve-386] Richard Hasen said the commission was supposed to give Trump cover to pass a national documentary proof of citizenship law.[^cite_note-\:9-166]

### 2020 election investigations

In the final weeks of 2020, the Trump campaign paid the [Berkeley Research Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berkeley_Research_Group) \$600,000 to investigate voter fraud claims in Georgia, Pennsylvania, Michigan, Wisconsin, Arizona and Nevada. The final report, dubbed "Project 2020"[^cite_note-387] undercut Trump's claims by failing to find widespread voter fraud and was never presented to the public or in court.[^cite_note-388]

The 2020 Trump campaign also paid a second firm, the data mining company [Simpatico Software Systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simpatico_Software_Systems), \$750,000 to study election fraud claims. Ken Block, the firm's founder, said that he was able to document and prove that every one of the claims was false.[^cite_note-389] "No substantive voter fraud was uncovered in my investigations looking for it." said Block.[^cite_note-390]

In December 2020, Attorney General [William Barr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Barr) told the [Associated Press](18935732.md) that federal prosecutors and the FBI looked into specific election integrity allegations and concluded that they had not found any evidence of widespread fraud.[^cite_note-391]

### Kurt Olsen and Mojave Research investigation (2025-2026)

In October 2025, the Trump administration hired former Trump campaign lawyer [Kurt Olsen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurt_Olsen) as a "special government employee" under the title "Director of Election Security and Integrity"[^cite_note-\:43-392] and tasked him with investigating fraud in the 2020 election.[^cite_note-393] At the direction of the president,[^cite_note-\:43-392] Olsen was given to access to highly classified intelligence material stored by U.S. spy agencies.[^cite_note-394] In May 2025, the administration seized Dominion voting machines in Puerto Rico in an attempt to prove a discredited conspiracy theory alleging vote-rigging by Venezuela. [Tulsi Gabbard](26328774.md), then [Director of National Intelligence](456082.md), contracted the cybersecurity firm Mojave Research to analyze the machines[^cite_note-\:05-395] and the firm submitted a report on their findings to her in July 2025. That report has yet to be made public, but sources have told press outlets that it failed to find evidence that weaknesses were exploited or that votes were altered.[^cite_note-\:05-395][^cite_note-396] Reuters reported that the failure to find evidence in Puerto Rico led the administration to expand the investigation to seize election ballots in Georgia, and subpoena voter records in Arizona.[^cite_note-\:05-395]

When confronted with the results of Mojave's investigation Olsen reportedly sent a message to Trump accusing the firm of serving the “[deep state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deep_state_conspiracy_theory_in_the_United_States)” and secretly taking money from [George Soros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Soros). The company denied the allegation and opened its books to show it took no money from Soros.[^cite_note-\:05-395]

### Homeland Security investigations (2025-2026)

Starting in the summer of 2025, Homeland Security Investigations, a law enforcement arm of the [US Immigration and Customs Enforcement](216537.md) agency, was tasked with finding election fraud cases in the United States, specifically looking for non-citizens who voted. The agency was given access to many state voter rolls, a move that some Department of Justice attorneys considered a breach of privacy and may have been illegal. As of May 2026, only 41 people were charged with voting illegally or other election-related crimes, and only 14 have been convicted.[^cite_note-397]

## Prevention

States each have different laws and methods to address electoral fraud. Some methods of preventing voter fraud have caused controversy due to their potential to disproportionately impact legal voters and/or certain demographic groups.[^cite_note-s458-398] A 2018 report released by the [National Academies of Sciences, Engineering, and Medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Academies_of_Sciences,_Engineering,_and_Medicine) recommended that states undergo regular [election audits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election_audit), cross-check voter registrations nationwide for duplicates, and adopt systems for voters to verify receipt and delivery of mail ballots. The report called for investments to improve election technology and administration; including technology to prevent coercion or vote buying with mail ballots, and identity verification technology at the polls such as [biometric markers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biometric_marker). It also recommended the use of [paper ballots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paper_ballot) and a ban on [internet voting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_voting).[^cite_note-z326-399]

### Voter ID laws

Main article: [Voter identification laws in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voter_identification_laws_in_the_United_States)

In the United States, voter ID laws (laws requiring [identification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Identity_document) to vote) have been enacted in 36 states as of 2024 with the stated aim of preventing voter impersonation.[^cite_note-\:1-400] They have mostly been introduced by Republican legislators since 2011.[^cite_note-c102-401][^cite_note-abc-61][^cite_note-columbialawreview_2014-65] Specific forms of ID required vary between states, with some requiring [photo identification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Photo_identification).[^cite_note-\:28-402] Some laws have been struck down in court as an undue burden.[^cite_note-\:1-400] Voter ID requirements are generally popular among Americans, though they are also a divisive issue.[^cite_note-l527-403][^cite_note-o528-404] Critics of voter ID laws have argued that they depress turnout by lawful voters under the pretense of addressing voter impersonation, which is quite rare.[^cite_note-\:1-400][^cite_note-columbialawreview_2014-65] Americans who have lower incomes, are younger or transgender are less likely to have an updated ID.[^cite_note-\:1-400][^cite_note-405]

### Citizenship verification

See also: [Voter identification laws in the United States § Existing processes to prevent noncitizen voting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voter_identification_laws_in_the_United_States#Existing_processes_to_prevent_noncitizen_voting)



Citizenship verification process in North Carolina as of 2017

[^cite_note-\:26-406]

The process of verifying the citizenship of voters varies by state.[^cite_note-Sherman_2020-129][^cite_note-x811-132] It is [best practice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Best_practice) for states to check registrations against [DMV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_motor_vehicles) or [Social Security](22255742.md) files to check for noncitizens.[^cite_note-\:24-110] Some methods states have tried have been discontinued due to errors or legal fights. Some states and counties like Kansas,[^cite_note-Sherman_2020-129] New Jersey[^cite_note-l383-407] and [Allegheny County, Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allegheny_County,_Pennsylvania)[^cite_note-o896-408] do not independently verify proof of citizenship.

According to the [North Carolina State Board of Elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Carolina_State_Board_of_Elections) in 2017, DMV data indicating noncitizenship is just the first step in determining someone's citizenship status because, "voters who appear to be non-citizens based on DMV data were confirmed to be U.S. citizens in the SAVE database 97.6 percent of the time."[^cite_note-\:26-406] If the status was then also listed as noncitizen in the [Systematic Alien Verifications for Entitlements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Systematic_Alien_Verification_for_Entitlements) database, the Board asked for more proof in mailings and interviews since 3/4 of those who had been able to provide proof of citizenship, remained listed in the SAVE database as noncitizens.[^cite_note-\:26-406]

Georgia compares voter rolls to [Social Security Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Security_Administration) and its Department of Driver Services, while Colorado uses the SAVE database as-needed.[^cite_note-Sherman-123] In Georgia, if someone's citizenship cannot be verified who registers to vote, they are put into a 'pending citizenship' status and prevented from registering.[^cite_note-\:24-110] Texas requires court clerks to notify the [secretary of state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_State_of_Texas) of those disqualified from [jury duty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jury_duty) for being noncitizens.[^cite_note-x811-132]

In Nevada, the state DMV does not pass along citizenship information to counties when they make voter registration checks, meaning that noncitizen registration is technically possible if applicants lie on their registration form.[^cite_note-o994-409] According to the [Bipartisan Policy Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bipartisan_Policy_Center) as of July 2024, access to federal citizenship data is "difficult, costly, and burdensome", and state election officials have "long struggled" to obtain it. Between January 2023 and July 2024, nine states instituted laws to solidify citizenship verification for voting and voter registration; mostly by improving data collaboration with state resources and federal databases.[^cite_note-f682-410]

#### Proof of citizenship laws

Main article: [Voter identification laws in the United States § Proof of citizenship laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voter_identification_laws_in_the_United_States#Proof_of_citizenship_laws)



Lack of citizenship documentation by political party (2024)

[^cite_note-\:2322-411]

[^cite_note-\:322-412]

Proof of citizenship laws require people who vote or register to vote to present documentary [proof of citizenship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proof_of_citizenship). Proponents have argued that they are necessary to prevent illegal noncitizen voting, while critics have said that noncitizen voting essentially does not occur and that the laws would disenfranchise large percentages of eligible voters who lack easy access to such documents, such as [college students](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_students), [tribal voters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tribe_\(Native_American\)), or low-income voters.[^cite_note-413][^cite_note-414][^cite_note-\:2322-411] A June 2024 Brennan Center study estimates that 21.3 million citizens (9% of voters) do not have easy access to documentary proof of citizenship, and that 3.8 million citizens lack access to any form, often because documents were lost, damaged or stolen.[^cite_note-Morris2024-415][^cite_note-\:322-412][^cite_note-\:42-416][^cite_note-\:9g-417][^cite_note-\:2322-411]

The legality of proof of citizenship laws has been disputed. In the 2013 *[Arizona v. Inter Tribal Council of Arizona, Inc.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arizona_v._Inter_Tribal_Council_of_Arizona,_Inc.)*, the [U.S. Supreme Court](31737.md) ruled that Arizona's proof of citizenship law violated the [1993 National Voter Registration Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Voter_Registration_Act_of_1993) for federal elections.[^cite_note-418] In August 2024, in *[Republican National Committee v. Mi Familia Vota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_National_Committee_v._Mi_Familia_Vota)* the Supreme Court allowed Arizona to enforce a law requiring proof of citizenship to register to vote using the state's registration forms (federal forms do not require documentation), pending appeal.[^cite_note-k946-419][^cite_note-420] In July 2024, the [United States House of Representatives](19468510.md) passed the [Safeguard American Voter Eligibility (SAVE) Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safeguard_American_Voter_Eligibility_\(SAVE\)_Act), which would mandate that Americans show documentary proof of citizenship when registering to vote and make it easier to sue election workers who register a noncitizen.[^cite_note-421][^cite_note-w107-422] The bill died after not being considered by the Senate.[^cite_note-423] The Bipartisan Policy Center argued there are better ways to ensure noncitizens do not vote, such as better data sharing between state departments, such as data provided to a DMV when attaining a [REAL ID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_ID_Act).[^cite_note-f682-410]

In the 2018 *[Fish v. Kobach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fish_v._Kobach)* case, U.S. District Court Judge [Julie Robinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julie_A._Robinson) ruled that [Kansas' proof of citizenship law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voter_identification_laws_in_the_United_States#Kansas) was unconstitutional, in part because the state did not demonstrate that any meaningful illegal noncitizen voting occurred and that 31,089 citizens without the right documentation had their voter registration cancelled or suspended.[^cite_note-z1542-424][^cite_note-Robinson2-425][^cite_note-426][^cite_note-\:13k2-427] In a 2023 case in Arizona, U.S. District Judge [Susan R. Bolton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susan_R._Bolton) struck down the documentary proof of citizenship requirement along similar lines but upheld some provisions, citing as credible estimates of 2022 noncitizen voting rates by [Jesse Richman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesse_Richman_\(academic\)), which other scholars have disputed.[^cite_note-Kessler_20242-44][^cite_note-428]

### Signature verification



Signature rejection thresholds by state (2023)

Further information: [Postal voting in the United States § Signature verification process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postal_voting_in_the_United_States#Signature_verification_process)

Signature verification is carried out by a majority of states in order to prevent forged paper ballots. According to the [Election Administration and Voting Survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election_Administration_and_Voting_Survey), 27.5% of rejected absentee ballots in 2016,[^cite_note-eac16-429] and 15.8%, of rejected mail-in ballots in 2018,[^cite_note-eac18-430] were due to signature mismatches. Tossing ballots due to signature mismatches can depend on the method of signature verification used.[^cite_note-431]

As of 2024, 31 states conduct signature verification on returned absentee or mail-in ballots. Nine states do not conduct signature verification, but require the signature of either a [witness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Witness), two witnesses, or a [notary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Notary). Ten states and [Washington, D.C.](108956.md), neither conduct signature verification nor require a witness signature.[^cite_note-u974-432] [Mississippi](16949861.md) is the only state to both conduct signature verification and require a witness signature (in this case, a notary).[^cite_note-u974-432] Four states ([Arkansas](1930.md), [Georgia](48830.md), [Minnesota](19590.md), and [Ohio](22199.md)) additionally require either a copy of the voter's ID or a voter identification number.[^cite_note-u974-432]

In 2024, in [Mesa County, Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesa_County,_Colorado), signature verification successfully detected around a dozen mail ballots that were stolen and fraudulently submitted on behalf of other voters. Three of the ballots had been wrongly accepted and counted after they were flagged by the automated system, but subsequently reviewed and approved by an election judge.[^cite_note-c614-83]

Experts have stated that disenfranchisement caused by mail-in ballots being discarded on technicalities, including non-matching or missing signatures, is a more pervasive problem than mail-in ballot fraud.[^cite_note-\:03-433][^cite_note-q853-434] Researchers at [Protect Democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protect_Democracy) found that "an explosion of misinformation" about how much cheating occurs among voters using mail-in ballots caused a spike in rejected signatures during the [2021 Georgia Senate runoffs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Georgia_Senate_runoffs) compared to the 2020 presidential election.[^cite_note-\:16-367]

### Marking ballots

Because it is standard procedure for ballots cast in-person to not contain [identifying information](1845896.md) about the voter, if an illegal in-person ballot is cast, it can be impossible to isolate it and prevent it from counting.[^cite_note-h677-435] Some states mark ballots with identifying numbers in certain circumstances. For example, an election worker in Maine will write a number on a ballot only if it is challenged,[^cite_note-h478-436] and election workers in North Carolina write identifying numbers on early in-person and mail-in ballots, which allows ballots to be retrieved and not counted if necessary.[^cite_note-i264-437]

### Election audits

Further information: [Election audit § Current practices in election results auditing in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election_audit#Current_practices_in_election_results_auditing_in_the_United_States)

As of 2024, 48 states conduct some type of [post-election audit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-election_audit), which check if the equipment and procedures used to count votes worked properly, and detect discrepancies using a [hand count](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hand_count) of paper records. The two exceptions are [Alabama](303.md) and [New Hampshire](21134.md), both of which nonetheless piloted different audit types in 2022. The type and scope of audit significantly varies between states.[^cite_note-z061-438]

### Voter roll management

Voter roll management seeks to balance sometimes competing interests of ensuring accurate voter rolls, protecting voter privacy and not disenfranchising or creating undue burdens on voters.[^cite_note-\:33-122]

#### Voter roll purges

See also: [Voter suppression in the United States § Purging of eligible voters from the rolls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voter_suppression_in_the_United_States#Purging_of_eligible_voters_from_the_rolls)

[Voter caging](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voter_caging) is the process of challenging the voter registration status of someone who is registered to vote. It often involves sending that person a postcard to the address on file and removing the voter if they do not respond within a certain time period.[^cite_note-439] The practice can be controversial with some civil rights groups successfully suing some states that target voters of a particular political party or race in such a way as to make it meaningfully impact election outcomes and voter's rights.[^cite_note-440]

#### Interstate databases

The [Interstate Voter Registration Crosscheck Program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_Voter_Registration_Crosscheck_Program) was a [database](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Database) established in 2005 and run by [Kansas](16716.md) that compared voting records across multiple states to prevent double voting. At least 28 states opted into the program, but academics and several states found that it returned high rates of [false positives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/False_positives) that would disenfranchise legal voters. Some states left as a result.[^cite_note-x247-441] In 2017, the program was put on hold after the [Department of Homeland Security](58236.md) discovered security vulnerabilities. In 2019, the program was indefinitely suspended as part of a settlement of a [class-action lawsuit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Class-action_lawsuit) filed by the [American Civil Liberties Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_Liberties_Union).[^cite_note-Hegeman-442] Earlier in 2012, the [Electronic Registration Information Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronic_Registration_Information_Center) (ERIC) was established with a goal of improving the accuracy of voter rolls through comparisons between states.[^cite_note-y860-443] At its peak, 33 states and the District of Columbia were members.[^cite_note-ncsleric-444] Beginning in 2022, nine Republican-led states left ERIC. States cited complaints about governance issues, including that ERIC mailed newly eligible voters who had not yet registered ahead of federal elections, and that it had become subject to alleged partisan influence.[^cite_note-m588-445][^cite_note-j399-446] ERIC was the subject of repeated false claims from allies of Donald Trump that it was a voter registration vehicle for Democrats. Several states that left ERIC subsequently created their own partnerships.[^cite_note-y860-443][^cite_note-m588-445][^cite_note-j399-446]

## Prosecution

In most states, a prosecutor must prove that an individual committed voter fraud intentionally or knowingly. In some states, however, any mistake on the part of a voter that leads to voting illegally can be grounds for prosecution.[^cite_note-o521-447] An election crime becomes a federal crime if the ballot includes one or more federal candidates, an election official is involved, election workers are threatened, or the crime relates to fraudulent voter registration or noncitizen voting. The [Federal Bureau of Investigation](11127.md) defines voter or ballot fraud as one of three broad categories of federal election crimes; the other two being [campaign finance violations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campaign_finance_in_the_United_States) and civil rights violations, such as voter intimidation or suppression.[^cite_note-b538-5]

According to *The* *New York Times*, prosecutions of voter fraud can lead to significantly varied outcomes depending on [socioeconomic status](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socioeconomic_status) and the state in which someone is being tried. Most violations "draw wrist-slaps", while some high-profile prosecutions have produced multiple-year jail terms.[^cite_note-i276-181] Often, prosecutions net people who did not realize they were breaking the law.[^cite_note-i276-181] Lorraine Minnite has argued that almost all cases of illegal voting are due to misunderstandings or administrative error,[^cite_note-\:21-39] which does not constitute [fraud](58790.md) in states where intent is required.[^cite_note-\:19-448] Prosecutions are exceedingly rare; as of 2022, an average of one and a half people per state per year were charged with voter fraud.[^cite_note-i276-181] Cases of voter fraud can be difficult to prove or prosecute,[^cite_note-v055-449][^cite_note-w661-450][^cite_note-a718-451] depending on the type of fraud alleged.[^cite_note-u302-59][^cite_note-r551-60][^cite_note-u448-452] According to [Bob Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Hall_\(American_political_journalist\)), former director of Democracy North Carolina, political will is especially required to investigate more complicated electoral fraud schemes, and prosecutors are more inclined to pursue easier cases such as when someone illegally votes while on probation.[^cite_note-t805-453]

Between 1970 and 2005, fewer than 40 people were convicted for illegal voting in Florida, and only two were sent to prison. The *[Tampa Bay Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tampa_Bay_Times)* attributed this to fraud largely being ignored by prosecutors "unless an election is questioned, someone complains or a voter is investigated on other charges".[^cite_note-h649-454] [Wisconsin Watch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Watch) evaluated voter fraud cases from 2012 to 2022 and found about 0.0006% of votes cast were challenged by a [district attorney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_attorney), with a voter's probation status as the most common reason.[^cite_note-455] A 2022 investigation by [KING-TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KING-TV) found that the likelihood of being charged for voter fraud in [Washington state](13015878.md) varied depending on the [county](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/County); [King County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_County), with a voting population of 1.3 million, had charged 9 cases of voter fraud since 2007, while the much smaller [Lewis County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lewis_County,_Washington) had charged 8 (at least 3 of which were dismissed).[^cite_note-q449-456] King County tended to write warning letters for isolated cases, such as a partner who cast the ballot of a recently deceased spouse, and focus prosecutions on cases where a repeat offense was more likely, such as canvassing fraud.[^cite_note-q449-456] The [United States Department of Justice](52563.md) publishes *Federal Prosecution of Election Offenses*, a handbook for district election officers. The 2017 edition warns against launching public investigations, without approval granted for extraordinary cases, into alleged fraud before an election is over so as not to tip the election with the publicity generated by an unfinished investigation.[^cite_note-k891-457][^cite_note-e152-458]

### Standing in lawsuits

In the aftermath of the 2020 presidential election, many courts ruled against plaintiffs in voter fraud lawsuits on the basis that they, as voters, lacked [standing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standing_\(law\)). According to [University of Memphis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Memphis) law professor Steven Mulroy, who was critical of some court dismissals based on standing, election cases raise unique standing challenges as the asserted harms are often split between many people.[^cite_note-i738-459] A novel type of vote dilution claim based on fraud has been employed by Republicans since 2020, and is based on the idea that election rules that make it too easy to cast fraudulent votes can dilute the strength of valid ones.[^cite_note-e979-460] Claims of this nature were not successful in 2020 though have since received some success in [federal district courts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_district_court).[^cite_note-z268-461]

Retrieved September 8, 2024. Mr. Richman still maintains that some small percentage of noncitizens vote in American elections. But the debate over this study has moved on. It's no longer about whether millions of illegal votes were cast, but whether there's any evidence for noncitizen voting at all. The study's bold claims fell apart because of something called response error: the possibility that people taking a survey don't answer a question correctly – in this case, a question about being American citizens. There is always a tiny amount of response error in surveys. Respondents might not understand the question. Or they might understand it, but mark the wrong answer by mistake, if the survey is self-administered. An interviewer, if there is one, could accidentally record the wrong answer. Such errors usually aren't a problem large enough to change the results of a survey.

Aside from larger databases, administrators may (for example) obtain from court authorities a list of persons who ask to be excused from jury duty on the grounds that they are not citizens. Not infrequently these people turn out to have been fibbing to the court clerk to get out of jury service and are in fact native-born citizens – thus generating a false positive. Other false positive matches can arise because someone omits to check the 'citizen' box on a driver's license application even though they are in fact a citizen, or because databases take a while to catch up after someone becomes a U.S. citizen through naturalization.  The more people believe elections are rigged, the more they are likely to turn their discontents in a direction other than electoral politics. Some will go the passive route of resignation, withdrawing from civic involvements, making themselves the perfect subjects for strongman rule. Others will turn to militia activity or outright violence.

Even when fraud was proven, the remedy could be hollow. In the Tenth Congressional District of Pennsylvania in 1918, the Democrat, Patrick McLane, was at first declared the winner, while the Republican, John R. Farr, contested the election. Over nearly two years, a Congressional committee examined the case; they determined in February 1921 that "wholesale fraud" had indeed cheated Farr out of his seat, and, by a 161 to 121 vote on the House floor, McLane was unseated and Farr sworn in to serve out the remainder of his term – six days.

[^cite_note-Waldman-383]: &#x20;[Michael Waldman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Waldman), [Donald Trump Tells His Voter Fraud Panel: Find Me 'Something'](http://www.brennancenter.org/blog/donald-trump-tells-his-voter-fraud-panel-find-me-%E2%80%98something%E2%80%99) [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20190911113804/https://www.brennancenter.org/blog/donald-trump-tells-his-voter-fraud-panel-find-me-%E2%80%98something%E2%80%99) September 11, 2019, at the [Wayback Machine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayback_Machine), [Brennan Center for Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brennan_Center_for_Justice), [New York University School of Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_University_School_of_Law) (July 20, 2017) (also republished at [The Daily Beast](http://www.thedailybeast.com/donald-trump-tells-his-voter-fraud-panel-find-me-something) [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20211024071215/https://www.thedailybeast.com/donald-trump-tells-his-voter-fraud-panel-find-me-something) October 24, 2021, at the [Wayback Machine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayback_Machine)): "The panel was created to justify one of the more outlandish presidential fibs ... After Trump was roundly mocked for his claim of 3 to 5 million illegal voters, the panel was launched in an effort to try to rustle up some evidence – any evidence – for the charge.... The purpose of the panel is not just to try to justify his laughable claims of millions of invisible illegal voters. It aims to stir fears, to lay the ground for new efforts to restrict voting. Trump's claims, after all, are just a cartoon version of the groundless arguments already used to justify restrictive voting laws."

[^cite_note-BermanWeigel-384]: &#x20;Mark Berman & [David Weigel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Weigel), [Trump's voting commission asked states to hand over election data. Some are pushing back.](https://www.washingtonpost.com/national/trumps-voting-commission-asked-states-to-hand-over-election-data-theyre-pushing-back/2017/06/30/cd8f812a-5dce-11e7-9b7d-14576dc0f39d_story.html) [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20201109032433/https://www.washingtonpost.com/national/trumps-voting-commission-asked-states-to-hand-over-election-data-theyre-pushing-back/2017/06/30/cd8f812a-5dce-11e7-9b7d-14576dc0f39d_story.html) November 9, 2020, at the [Wayback Machine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayback_Machine), *Washington Post* (June 30, 2017): "Experts described the request as ... a recipe for potential voter suppression.... 'This is an attempt on a grand scale to purport to match voter rolls with other information in an apparent effort to try and show that the voter rolls are inaccurate and use that as a pretext to pass legislation that will make it harder for people to register to vote,' said [Rick Hasen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rick_Hasen), an election-law expert at the University of California, Irvine. Hasen said he has "no confidence" in whatever results the committee produces. He said the commission and its request create a number of concerns, including that it is an election group created by one candidate for office – Trump, who already is campaigning for reelection – and headed by Pence, another political candidate. 'It's just a recipe for a biased and unfair report,' Hasen said. "And it's completely different from the way that every other post-election commission has been done."

[^cite_note-e979-460]: &#x20;Stephanopoulos, Nicholas O. (October 29, 2021). ["The New Vote Dilution"](https://nyulawreview.org/wp-content/uploads/2021/10/Stephanopoulos-ONLINE.pdf) (PDF). *NYU Law Review*. **96**: 1179–1213. Retrieved October 18, 2024. In a barrage of lawsuits about the 2020 election, conservative plaintiffs argued that electoral policies that make it easier to vote are unconstitutionally dilutive. Their logic was that (1) these policies enable fraud through their lack of proper safeguards and (2) the resulting fraudulent votes dilute the ballots cast by law-abiding citizens.

[^cite_note-z268-461]: &#x20;Marisam, Jason (March 26, 2024). ["Fraudulent Vote Dilution"](https://ir.lawnet.fordham.edu/cgi/viewcontent.cgi?article=1032\&context=vrdf). *Fordham Law Voting Rights and Democracy Forum*. **2** (2). Retrieved October 18, 2024. In 2020, the Republican Party and the Trump campaign brought a series of lawsuits that advanced a novel type of voting claim, which this Article calls fraudulent vote dilution. This claim asserts that an election rule is unconstitutional because it makes it too easy to cast fraudulent ballots that, when tabulated, will dilute the strength of valid and honest ballots. While these claims were not successful in 2020, in the years since, fraudulent vote dilution theories have gained some traction in federal district courts.
